Telmisartan; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Monitor for signs and symptoms of hyponatremia during concomitant diuretic and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor (SNRI) use; consider discontinuing the SNRI if symptomatic hyponatremia occurs and institute appropriate medical intervention. Concomitant use increases the risk for developing hyponatremia.
Tenecteplase: (Moderate) Monitor for bleeding during concomitant use of serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), such as venlafaxine, and medications that interfere with hemostasis, such as thrombolytic agents. SNRIs affect platelet activation and may interfere with clot formation increasing the risk for bleeding.
Tetrabenazine: (Major) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and tetrabenazine increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Thioridazine: (Contraindicated) Venlafaxine is contraindicated for use with thioridazine. Venlafaxine is associated with a possible risk of QT prolongation. Thioridazine has an established risk of QT prolongation and torsades de pointes (TdP). In addition, venlafaxine impairs the activity of CYP2D6, and the use of thioridazine concomitantly with CYP2D6 inhibitors is contraindicated due to the theoretical risk of prolongation of QTc interval and subsequent arrhythmias due to elevated serum concentrations of thioridazine.
Thrombolytic Agents: (Moderate) Monitor for bleeding during concomitant use of serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), such as venlafaxine, and medications that interfere with hemostasis, such as thrombolytic agents. SNRIs affect platelet activation and may interfere with clot formation increasing the risk for bleeding.
Ticagrelor: (Moderate) Monitor for bleeding during concomitant use of serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), such as venlafaxine, and medications that interfere with hemostasis, such as ticagrelor. SNRIs affect platelet activation and may interfere with clot formation increasing the risk for bleeding.
Tirofiban: (Moderate) Monitor for bleeding during concomitant use of serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), such as venlafaxine, and medications that interfere with hemostasis, such as tirofiban. SNRIs affect platelet activation and may interfere with clot formation increasing the risk for bleeding.
Tolmetin: (Moderate) Platelet aggregation may be impaired by venlafaxine due to platelet serotonin depletion, possibly increasing the risk of a bleeding complication (e.g., gastrointestinal bleeding, ecchymoses, epistaxis, hematomas, petechiae, hemorrhage) in patients receiving nonsteroidal antiinflammatory drugs (NSAIDs). Monitor patients for signs and symptoms of bleeding when coadministering venlafaxine with NSAIDs.
Tolterodine: (Moderate) Venlafaxine may inhibit the CYP2D6 metabolism of tolterodine in extensive metabolizers. In addition, tolterodine is associated with dose-dependent prolongation of the QT interval, especially in poor metabolizers of CYP2D6. Venlafaxine is also associated with QT prolongation. Use tolterodine and venlafaxine concomitantly with caution.
Toremifene: (Major) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and toremifene increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Torsemide: (Moderate) Monitor for signs and symptoms of hyponatremia during concomitant diuretic and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor (SNRI) use; consider discontinuing the SNRI if symptomatic hyponatremia occurs and institute appropriate medical intervention. Concomitant use increases the risk for developing hyponatremia.
traMADol: (Moderate) Monitor for reduced efficacy of tramadol, signs of opioid withdrawal, seizures, or serotonin syndrome if coadministration with venlafaxine is necessary. If venlafaxine is discontinued, consider a dose reduction of tramadol and frequently monitor for signs of respiratory depression and sedation. Tramadol is a CYP2D6 substrate and venlafaxine is a CYP2D6 inhibitor. Concomitant use of tramadol with CYP2D6 inhibitors can increase the plasma concentration of tramadol and decrease the plasma concentration of the active metabolite M1. Since M1 is a more potent mu-opioid agonist, decreased M1 exposure could result in decreased therapeutic effects, and may result in signs and symptoms of opioid withdrawal in patients who have developed physical dependence to tramadol. Increased tramadol exposure can result in increased or prolonged therapeutic effects and increased risk for serious adverse events including seizures and serotonin syndrome. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
Tramadol; Acetaminophen: (Moderate) Monitor for reduced efficacy of tramadol, signs of opioid withdrawal, seizures, or serotonin syndrome if coadministration with venlafaxine is necessary. If venlafaxine is discontinued, consider a dose reduction of tramadol and frequently monitor for signs of respiratory depression and sedation. Tramadol is a CYP2D6 substrate and venlafaxine is a CYP2D6 inhibitor. Concomitant use of tramadol with CYP2D6 inhibitors can increase the plasma concentration of tramadol and decrease the plasma concentration of the active metabolite M1. Since M1 is a more potent mu-opioid agonist, decreased M1 exposure could result in decreased therapeutic effects, and may result in signs and symptoms of opioid withdrawal in patients who have developed physical dependence to tramadol. Increased tramadol exposure can result in increased or prolonged therapeutic effects and increased risk for serious adverse events including seizures and serotonin syndrome. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
Tranylcypromine: (Contraindicated) Due to the risk of serotonin syndrome, monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) intended to treat psychiatric disorders are contraindicated for use with serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs). MAOIs should not be used within 5 days of discontinuing treatment with duloxetine or milnacipran or within 7 days of discontinuing treatment with other SNRIs. Conversely, SNRIs should not be initiated within 14 days of stopping an MAOI. Monitor the patient for serotonin-related effects during therapy transitions.
traZODone: (Major) Due to the potential for QT prolongation, cautious use and close monitoring are advisable if concurrent use of trazodone and venlafaxine is necessary. Both drugs may cause QT interval prolongation and a risk for torsade de pointes (TdP). In addition, concurrent use of trazodone with other drugs that modulate serotonergic function, such as venlafaxine, has resulted in serotonin syndrome in some cases. Patients should be carefully observed, particularly during treatment initiation and during dose adjustments. Discontinue the serotonergic medications if serotonin syndrome is suspected.
Triamterene: (Moderate) Monitor for signs and symptoms of hyponatremia during concomitant diuretic and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor (SNRI) use; consider discontinuing the SNRI if symptomatic hyponatremia occurs and institute appropriate medical intervention. Concomitant use increases the risk for developing hyponatremia.
Triamterene; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Monitor for signs and symptoms of hyponatremia during concomitant diuretic and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor (SNRI) use; consider discontinuing the SNRI if symptomatic hyponatremia occurs and institute appropriate medical intervention. Concomitant use increases the risk for developing hyponatremia.
Triclabendazole: (Moderate) Concomitant use of triclabendazole and venlafaxine may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Tricyclic antidepressants: (Major) The combination of venlafaxine and tricyclic antidepressants may lead to serotonin syndrome and increased plasma concentrations of TCAs. Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) with other drugs that have serotonergic properties such as tricyclic antidepressants. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Venlafaxine is an inhibitor of CYP2D6, and many TCAs are metabolized by this isozyme. One case report documented a first-time seizure in a patient receiving venlafaxine and trimipramine at therapeutic dosages.
Trifluoperazine: (Moderate) Caution is advisable during concurrent use of trifluoperazine and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) since elevations in plasma concentrations of trifluoperazine may occur. Phenothiazines are CYP2D6 substrates, and SNRIs including duloxetine and venlafaxine are CYP2D6 inhibitors. In addition, both venlafaxine and trifluoperazine are associated with a possible risk of QT prolongation; therefore, additive cardiac effects are possible. Although clinical studies have shown that desvenlafaxine does not have a clinically relevant effect on CYP2D6 inhibition at doses of 100 mg/day, the manufacturer recommends that doses of medications that are primary substrates of CYP2D6 be reduced by one-half during co-administration of desvenlafaxine doses of 400 mg/day.
Trimipramine: (Major) The combination of venlafaxine and tricyclic antidepressants may lead to serotonin syndrome and increased plasma concentrations of TCAs. Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) with other drugs that have serotonergic properties such as tricyclic antidepressants. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Venlafaxine is an inhibitor of CYP2D6, and many TCAs are metabolized by this isozyme. One case report documented a first-time seizure in a patient receiving venlafaxine and trimipramine at therapeutic dosages.
Triptorelin: (Moderate) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., triptorelin) may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Tryptophan, 5-Hydroxytryptophan: (Major) The manufacturer of venlafaxine recommends against concurrent use with tryptophan when possible. Since tryptophan is converted to serotonin (5-hydroxytryptamine), the use of tryptophan in patients receiving venlafaxine could lead to serotonin excess and serotonin syndrome. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome.
Valerian, Valeriana officinalis: (Moderate) The German Commission E and other groups warn that any substances that act on the CNS, including psychopharmacologic agents like venlafaxine, may interact with the phytomedicinal valerian, Valeriana officinalis. These interactions are probably pharmacodynamic in nature, or result from additive mechanisms of action.
Valsartan; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Monitor for signs and symptoms of hyponatremia during concomitant diuretic and serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor (SNRI) use; consider discontinuing the SNRI if symptomatic hyponatremia occurs and institute appropriate medical intervention. Concomitant use increases the risk for developing hyponatremia.
Vandetanib: (Major) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and vandetanib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Vardenafil: (Moderate) Concomitant use of vardenafil and venlafaxine may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Vemurafenib: (Major) Vemurafenib has been associated with QT prolongation. If vemurafenib and another drug, such as venlafaxine, that is associated with a possible risk for QT prolongation and torsade de pointes must be coadministered, ECG monitoring is recommended; closely monitor the patient for QT interval prolongation. Also, venlafaxine is a substrate of CYP2D6 and 3A4, while vemurafenib is a weak CYP2D6 inhibitor and CYP3A4 substrate/inducer. Therefore, altered concentrations of venlafaxine may occur. Monitor the patient for toxicity and efficacy.
Vepdegestrant: (Major) Concomitant use of vepdegestrant and venlafaxine increases the risk for QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) and may increase venlafaxine exposure and the risk for other venlafaxine-related adverse effects. Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. If concomitant use is necessary, consider a venlafaxine dosage reduction. Additionally, consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring. Venlafaxine is a CYP3A substrate, vepdegestrant is a weak CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another weak CYP3A inhibitor increased venlafaxine overall exposure by approximately 1.5-fold.
Vilazodone: (Major) Due to possible additive effects on serotonin concentrations, it is advisable to avoid combining serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) with vilazodone. Interactions between vilazodone and serotonergic agents can lead to serotonin syndrome. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by the rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Patients receiving vilazodone and an SNRI should be monitored for the emergence of serotonin syndrome, particularly during treatment initiation and during dosage increases. Vilazodone and the SNRI should be discontinued if serotonin syndrome occurs and supportive symptomatic treatment should be initiated.
Viloxazine: (Moderate) Monitor for an increase in venlafaxine-related adverse effects if concomitant use of viloxazine is necessary. Concomitant use may increase venlafaxine exposure; viloxazine is a weak CYP2D6 inhibitor and venlafaxine is a CYP2D6 substrate. Since both drugs may increase blood pressure via blocking of norepinephrine reuptake, blood pressure and heart rate should be monitored periodically.
Voclosporin: (Moderate) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and voclosporin may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. The degree of QT prolongation associated with voclosporin is not clinically significant when administered within the recommended dosage range; QT prolongation has been described at 3 times the maximum recommended dose.
Vonoprazan; Amoxicillin; Clarithromycin: (Major) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and clarithromycin increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Vorapaxar: (Moderate) Monitor for bleeding during concomitant use of serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), such as venlafaxine, and medications that interfere with hemostasis, such as vorapaxar. SNRIs affect platelet activation and may interfere with clot formation increasing the risk for bleeding.
Voriconazole: (Major) Caution is advised when administering voriconazole with venlafaxine due to the potential for additive effects on the QT interval and increased exposure to venlafaxine. Both drugs are associated with QT prolongation; coadministration may increase this risk. Voriconazole has also been associated with rare cases of torsades de pointes, cardiac arrest, and sudden death. In addition, venlafaxine is a substrate of CYP2D6 (major) and CYP3A4 (minor). In patients who are poor CYP2D6 metabolizers, the CYP3A4 pathway for venlafaxine may become more important. Administration of venlafaxine and voriconazole (a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor) to patients identified as CYP2D6 poor metabolizers may significantly increase venlafaxine plasma concentrations. If these drugs are given together, closely monitor for prolongation of the QT interval. Rigorous attempts to correct any electrolyte abnormalities (i.e., potassium, magnesium, calcium) should be made before initiating concurrent therapy.
Vorinostat: (Moderate) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and vorinostat may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Vortioxetine: (Major) Due to similarity of pharmacology and the potential for additive adverse effects, including serotonin syndrome, vortioxetine should generally not be administered with serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) such as venlafaxine, desvenlafaxine, duloxetine, and milnacipran. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome. If serotonin syndrome is suspected, vortioxetine and concurrent serotonergic agents should be discontinued.
Warfarin: (Moderate) Advise patients of the increased bleeding risk associated with the concomitant use of venlafaxine and warfarin. Carefully monitor patients receiving warfarin therapy, including coagulation parameters such as the INR and/or prothrombin time, if venlafaxine is initiated or discontinued. Case reports and epidemiological studies have demonstrated an association between use of drugs that interfere with serotonin reuptake and gastrointestinal bleeding.
Ziftomenib: (Major) Concomitant use of ziftomenib and venlafaxine increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Ziprasidone: (Major) Concomitant use of ziprasidone and venlafaxine increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
ZOLMitriptan: (Moderate) Monitor for signs and symptoms of serotonin syndrome, particularly during treatment initiation and dosage increase, during concomitant venlafaxine and serotonin-receptor agonist use. If serotonin syndrome occurs, discontinue therapy. Concomitant use increases the risk for serotonin syndrome.
Zolpidem: (Moderate) Disorientation, delusions, or hallucinations have been reported rarely during co-administration of zolpidem and antidepressants. The duration of the visual hallucinations has ranged from 30 minutes to 7 hours. The interaction is thought to be pharmacodynamic in nature; therefore, a similar reaction is possible with venlafaxine. Data from a clinical study in which SSRI-treated patients were given immediate-release zolpidem revealed that four of the seven discontinuations during double-blind treatment with zolpidem (n = 95) were associated with impaired concentration, continuing or aggravated depression, and manic reaction.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  In clinical trials, common CNS adverse events observed with venlafaxine at an incidence >= 5% and twice that of placebo included drowsiness (23%, vs. 9% placebo).
 
In clinical trials, common CNS adverse events observed with venlafaxine at an incidence >= 5% and twice that of placebo included asthenia (weakness) (15%, vs. 3% placebo). Asthenia resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients.
 
In clinical trials, common CNS adverse events observed with venlafaxine at an incidence >= 5% and twice that of placebo included dizziness (19%, vs. 7% placebo). Over a period of 6 weeks, some patients adapted to the dizziness.
 
In clinical trials, common CNS adverse events observed with venlafaxine at an incidence >= 5% and twice that of placebo included xerostomia (22%, vs. 11% placebo). Xerostomia resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients and, over a period of 6 weeks, patients did not adapt to this side effect.
 
In clinical trials, common CNS adverse events observed with venlafaxine at an incidence >= 5% and twice that of placebo included: insomnia (15—23% vs. roughly 10%) and nervousness/anxiety (13/6% vs. 6/3%). Anxiety, insomnia, and nervousness resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients.
 
Although the incidences are too low to determine causality, dyskinesia and tardive dyskinesia have been documented in post-marketing reports with venlafaxine.
 
All effective antidepressants can precipitate mania in predisposed individuals. In pre-marketing depression trials, mania or hypomania occurred in 0.5% of patients treated with immediate-release venlafaxine compared with 0% of placebo-treated patients; however, the incidence is reportedly less with other indications. Mania or hypomania occurred in 0.3% of patients treated with extended-release venlafaxine for depression versus none in the placebo group. If mania occurs, the antidepressant should be withheld and appropriate therapy initiated to treat the manic symptoms.
 
Suicidal ideation has been reported in antidepressant clinical trials. A causal role has been established for antidepressants in inducing suicidality in children and young adults (18 to 24 years of age) during the early phase of treatment. Symptoms including aggressiveness, akathisia (psychomotor restlessness), agitation, anxiety, insomnia, irritability, hostility, mania, hypomania, impulsivity, and panic attacks have been reported in adult and pediatric patients being treated with antidepressants for major depressive disorder as well as for other indications, both psychiatric and nonpsychiatric. Although a causal link between the emergence of such symptoms and either the worsening of depression and/or the emergence of suicidal impulses has not been established, there is concern that such symptoms may represent precursors to emerging suicidality. Consideration should be given to changing the therapeutic regimen, including possibly discontinuing the medication, in patients whose depression is persistently worse or who are experiencing emergent suicidality or symptoms that might be precursors to worsening depression or suicidal ideation. A change or discontinuation of the therapeutic regimen should especially be considered if these symptoms are severe, abrupt in onset, or were not part of the patients presenting symptoms. Many of these adverse symptoms have been reported in clinical trials and have not always been associated with suicidality. Rapid discontinuation of antidepressants may also result in many of these adverse CNS events.
 
In clinical trials, common CNS adverse events observed with venlafaxine at an incidence >= 5% and twice that of placebo included tremor (5% vs. 1% placebo). Tremor resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients. A dose-related effect on tremor was seen during long-term clinical trials with extended-release venlafaxine in patients with social anxiety disorder.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that centrally-mediated effects including catatonia, impaired coordination and balance, and delirium have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established. Altered levels of consciousness have occurred in conjunction with overdose of venlafaxine in some cases.
 
Pre-existing high blood pressure should be controlled prior to beginning treatment with venlafaxine. Venlafaxine has been associated with dose-related hypertension. Hypertension cases that required immediate treatment have been reported post-approval. Venlafaxine can induce a sustained dose-related hypertension (typically 10—15 mm Hg). During pre-marketing studies with extended-release venlafaxine, the percentages of patients with various psychiatric diagnoses having sustained hypertension, defined as supine diastolic blood pressure (SDBP) of at least 90 mm Hg and at least 10 mm Hg above baseline during consecutive visits, were as follows: major depressive disorder (3%), generalized anxiety disorder (0.5%), social anxiety disorder (0.6%), and panic disorder (0.9%). The percentage of patients with sustained elevations in SDBP receiving immediate-release venlafaxine was 3% of those taking less than 100 mg/day and 13% of those taking more than 300 mg/day. In trials, hypertension was recorded in 2.2% of patients receiving immediate-release venlafaxine doses of 225 mg (n=89) and 4.5% receiving 375 mg (n=88). Across all clinical trials with extended-release venlafaxine, 1.4% of patients experienced an increase in SDBP of at least 15 mm Hg compared to 0.9% of those receiving placebo. Regular monitoring of blood pressure is recommended for all patients. Blood pressure increases may necessitate a dose reduction or possible discontinuation of venlafaxine.
 
In venlafaxine trials, analysis of ECG changes in 769 patients showed statistically significant and dose-related changes in heart rate (relative increase of 4—8 beats/min). In clinical trials for major depressive disorder, QT prolongation was documented with a mean increase in QTc of 4.7 msec for extended-release venlafaxine vs. a decrease of 1.9 msec for placebo. In clinical trials for social anxiety disorder, the mean increase in QTc interval in patients treated with extended-release venlafaxine was 3.4 msec vs. a decrease of 1.6 msec in those receiving placebo. Post-marketing reports indicate that increased CPK, cardiac arrhythmias (e.g., atrial fibrillation ventricular extrasystoles, ventricular fibrillation, ventricular tachycardia, supraventricular tachycardia (SVT)), QT prolongation, and torsade de pointes have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established. Electrocardiogram (ECG) changes such as prolongation of QT interval, bundle branch block, QRS prolongation, sinus tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, and sinus bradycardia have occurred in conjunction with overdose of venlafaxine in some cases.
 
Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse effects of venlafaxine are very common. In trials, nausea/vomiting (37%/6%, vs. 11%/2% placebo) were frequently reported and resulted in venlafaxine discontinuation for some patients. Over several weeks, patients may adapt to the GI effects of venlafaxine.
 
Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse effects of venlafaxine are very common. In trials, constipation (15%, vs. 7% placebo), was frequently reported and resulted in venlafaxine discontinuation for some patients. Over several weeks, patients may adapt to the GI effects of venlafaxine.
 
Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse effects of venlafaxine are very common. In clinical trials for social anxiety disorder, treatment-emergent dyspepsia was reported in 7% of patients receiving extended-release venlafaxine versus 6% of those receiving placebo. Over several weeks, patients may adapt to the GI effects of venlafaxine.
 
Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse effects of venlafaxine are very common. In clinical trials, anorexia (11%, vs. 2% placebo) was frequently reported and resulted in venlafaxine discontinuation for some patients. Over several weeks, patients may adapt to the GI effects of venlafaxine. Venlafaxine also leads to a dose-dependent weight loss in some patients. Weight loss occurring with venlafaxine is not always associated with treatment-induced anorexia. In adult major depression disorder (MDD) clinical trials, about 5% of body weight was lost in 7% of patients receiving venlafaxine XR, compared to 2% of patients receiving placebo. The discontinuation rate due to weight loss in depression trials was low, roughly 0.1% of patients. Weight loss in adult general anxiety disorder (GAD) trials (lasting up to 6 months) was similar to the weight loss in depression trials. Weight loss in panic and social anxiety disorder trials was slightly less than that seen in depression and GAD trials, occurring in 4% of those receiving venlafaxine and 1% of those receiving placebo. Weight loss has also been noted with pediatric use of venlafaxine. In 8-week studies for MDD or GAD, venlafaxine-treated pediatric patients lost an average of 0.45 kg while placebo-treated patients gained an average of 0.77 kg. Eighteen percent of children in the venlafaxine group lost >= 3.5% of body weight compared to 3.6% of the placebo group, a significant difference. In social anxiety disorder (SAD) studies, pediatric patients lost on average 0.75 kg while placebo groups gained roughly the same amount. Additionally, 47% of venlafaxine-treated children lost >= 3.5% of their body weight compared to 14% of placebo patients. Six month studies in children with MDD have revealed that longer term weight gains are less than expected based on data from age- and sex-matched peers. The difference between observed weight gain and expected weight gain was larger for children than for adolescents. Changes in height have also been noted in short and long-term studies. In an 8-week study, pediatric patients (ages 6—17) grew an average of 0.3 cm (n = 122), while placebo-treated patients grew an average of 1.0 cm (n = 132); the difference was significant. This difference in height was most notable in patients 12 years of age. During a 16-week, placebo-controlled SAD study, both the venlafaxine XR-treated (n = 109) and the placebo-treated (n = 112) patients each grew an average of 1 cm. A six month open-label study also noted height increases that were less than expected based on data from age- and sex-matched peers; however, the long-term consequences are not known. Clinicians should regularly monitor height and weight changes in pediatric patients receiving venlafaxine.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that shock-like electrical sensations and tinnitus have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established. In some cases, these reactions have occurred in conjunction with treatment discontinuation or tapering of the venlafaxine dose.
 
Venlafaxine therapy may cause sexual dysfunction. Libido decrease occurred in 2% of all patients. Ejaculation dysfunction occurred in up to 16% and impotence (erectile dysfunction) in up to 10% of male patients taking part in venlafaxine trials (with placebo rates consistently = 50 mg/dl from baseline and to a value >= 261 mg/dl) in 5.3% of venlafaxine-treated patients vs. 0% of placebo-treated patients. With at least 3 months of venlafaxine treatment in a 12-month study, the mean elevation in serum cholesterol was 9.1 mg/dl vs. a decrease of 7.1 mg/dl in the placebo group. Mean increases in serum cholesterol concentrations of 7.9 mg/dl and 5.6 mg/dl occurred in clinical trials of 12 weeks and 6 months duration, respectively, with extended-release venlafaxine. In the same study, a mean decrease in serum cholesterol concentrations was observed in the placebo group. The increases in serum cholesterol were dose and duration dependent. The manufacturer lists hypercholesterolemia as an infrequent adverse event (occurring in 1/100 to 1/1000 patients). The clinician may wish to monitor serum cholesterol levels at baseline and periodically during treatment with venlafaxine.
 
Musculoskeletal system disorders, such as rhabdomyolysis, have been documented in spontaneous post-marketing reports with venlafaxine use, but causality cannot be determined due to the low incidence.
 
A neonatal abstinence syndrome has been reported in infants exposed to serotonergic agents in utero. After birth, symptoms consistent with withdrawal (i.e., poor feeding, hypoglycemia, hypothermia, lethargy or irritability, vomiting, etc.) were noted. Such complications can arise immediately upon delivery. Other symptoms have included respiratory distress, cyanosis, apnea, seizures, temperature instability, feeding difficulty, hypotonia, hypertonia, hyperreflexia, tremor, jitteriness, and constant crying. Serum concentrations of the serotonergic agent were measurable in the infants affected. Several other symptoms (bloody stools, necrotizing enterocolitis) may have been attributable to rebound platelet activation on withdrawal of the exposure to the SSRI. Neonatal symptoms generally improved over several days. Additionally, a cohort study of 55 women revealed that 22% (12/55) of neonates exposed to an SSRI in the third trimester had complications requiring treatment or extended hospitalization compared with 6% in comparison groups. Complications included respiratory distress (n=9), hypoglycemia (n=2) and jaundice (n=1). The incidence of prematurity in the third trimester SSRI group was significant at 20% vs. 3.7% of controls. These features are consistent with either a direct toxic effect of serotonergic agents, or, possibly, a drug discontinuation syndrome. It should be noted that, in some cases, the clinical picture is consistent with serotonin syndrome. When treating a pregnant woman with an SSRI or other serotonergic agent during the third trimester, the physician should carefully consider the potential risks and benefits of treatment. If clinically feasible, and taking the drug half-life into consideration, tapering of the serotonergic agent prior to delivery may be considered as an alternative.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that congenital anomalies (unspecified) have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that pancreatitis has occurred. The incidence is unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.
 
Elevated hepatic enzymes (ALT, AST, GGT, and alkaline phosphatase) and probable drug-induced hepatitis were described in a case report of a woman aged 60 years who received venlafaxine 75 mg PO daily for vasomotor symptoms. After roughly one month of therapy, the patient presented with abdominal pain and an enlarged liver. Venlafaxine was discontinued, and after 1 week the liver function tests improved. After 4 weeks, a rechallenge was attempted with 37.5 mg venlafaxine PO daily; however, liver enzymes also increased with the lower dose. Post-marketing reports indicate that GGT elevation, LDH elevation, abnormal liver function tests (unspecified), hepatic necrosis, hepatic failure, and fatty liver have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established. Health care providers should be vigilant for the possibility of drug-induced hepatitis in patients receiving venlafaxine.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that interstitial lung disease and eosinophilic pneumonia have occurred rarely. A causal relationship to these complications has not been established; however, symptoms including cough, dyspnea, or chest discomfort should be promptly evaluated. Discontinuation of venlafaxine may become necessary.
 
In placebo-controlled clinical trials for social anxiety disorder, yawning occurred in 5% of patients receiving extended-release venlafaxine versus less than 1% of those receiving placebo. In a separate trial, this effect was found to be dose-related, occurring twice as often in patients receiving venlafaxine 150—225 mg/day compared to those receiving 75 mg/day.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that agranulocytosis, aplastic anemia, neutropenia, and pancytopenia have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that toxic epidermal necrolysis, Stevens-Johnson Syndrome, and erythema multiforme have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that anaphylactoid reactions have occurred. The incidences are unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.
 
Post-marketing reports indicate that renal failure (unspecified) has occurred. The incidence is unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    agitation, anxiety, confusion, delirium, dizziness, drowsiness, emotional lability, euphoria, hallucinations, headache, hostility, impulse control symptoms, insomnia, paranoia, psychosis, yawning
    General Information
    Common neuropsychiatric adverse events observed with immediate-release (IR) and extended-release venlafaxine for various psychiatric indications, and reported at incidences higher than with placebo, included: headache (25% IR; 38% ER), insomnia (18% to 23% IR; 15% to 24% ER), drowsiness (23% IR; 12% to 20% ER), dizziness (19% IR; 11% to 20% ER), nervousness (13% IR; 6% to 10% ER), anxiety (6% IR; 5% ER), agitation (2% IR; 3% ER), confusion (2% IR only), and abnormal dreams (4% IR; 3% to 7% ER). Agitation, dizziness, and somnolence appear to be dose-related. Anxiety, insomnia, and nervousness resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients. Yawning occurred in 3% to 5% of patients receiving ER venlafaxine during pre-marketing studies. In a separate trial, yawning was found to be dose-related, occurring twice as often, in up to 8% of patients receiving venlafaxine ER 150 to 225 mg/day or IR 375 mg/day compared to those receiving 75 mg/day. Infrequent (0.1% to 1%) psychiatric reactions reported during the pre-marketing evaluation of venlafaxine include emotional lability, euphoria, hallucinations, hostility, psychosis, and stupor. Rare (less than 0.1%) events included alcohol abuse, loss of consciousness, delusions, dementia, feeling drunk, impulse control symptoms (impulsivity, less than 0.1%), and paranoia reaction. Delirium has been reported postmarketing. Altered levels of consciousness have occurred in conjunction with overdose of venlafaxine in some cases.

    Headline
    akathisia, akinesia, amnesia, aphasia, asthenia, ataxia, dysarthria, dystonic reaction, Guillain-Barre syndrome, hyperesthesia, hyperkinesis, hyperreflexia, hypertonia, hyporeflexia, hypotonia, muscle paralysis, neuritis, neuropathic pain, nystagmus, paresis, paresthesias, peripheral neuropathy, restlessness, stroke, torticollis, tremor, trismus, vertigo, weakness
    General Information
    In placebo-controlled clinical trials of IR and ER venlafaxine (various psychiatric indications) asthenia (weakness) was reported in 8% to 15% IR and 8% to 19% ER and at higher rates than with placebo. Asthenia resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients. Hypertonia was reported in 3% of adults receiving venlafaxine IR and ER. Paresthesias were reported in 3% of patients during trials of venlafaxine IR and 2% to 3% of those taking venlafaxine ER. Twitching occured with IR venlafaxine (1%) and ER venlafaxine (3%). Tremor was reported with venlafaxine IR (5%) and in 4% to 5% with venlafaxine ER and resulted in discontinuation of therapy for some patients. A dose-related effect on tremor was seen during some long-term clinical trials with ER venlafaxine. Trismus and vertigo were reported in 1% or more of patients during premarketing clinical evaluations of IR and ER venlafaxine; amnesia, hypethesia, and abnormal thinking were reported in 1% or more of those taking venlafaxine ER. Infrequent (0.1% to 1%) events included akathisia (psychomotor restlessness), ataxia, circumoral paresthesia, CNS stimulation, hyperesthesia, hyperkinesis, hypotonia, incoordination, myoclonus, neuralgia or neuropathic pain, peripheral neuropathy, abnormal speech (dysarthria) and stupor. Rare (less than 0.1%) events included akinesia, aphasia, bradykinesia, buccoglossal syndrome, cerebrovascular accident (stroke), dystonic reaction, facial muscle paralysis, feeling drunk, abnormal gait or gait imbalance, Guillain-Barre syndrome, hypokinesia, neuritis, nystagmus, paresis, reflexes decreased (hyporeflexia), reflexes increased (hyperreflexia), and torticollis.

    Headline
    dyskinesia, tardive dyskinesia
    General Information
    Although the incidences are too low to determine causality, dyskinesia and tardive dyskinesia have been documented in postmarketing reports with venlafaxine.

    Headline
    depression, mania, suicidal ideation
    General Information
    Antidepressants can precipitate mania, hypomania, or other severe, unusual changes in moods or behaviors, especially in predisposed individuals; mania or hypomania occurred in 0.3% to 0.5% of venlafaxine-treated patients during premarketing trials. Monitor all antidepressant-treated patients for any indication for worsening of depression or the condition being treated and the emergence of suicidal behaviors or suicidal ideation, especially during the initial few months of drug therapy and after dosage changes. Depression or worsening of depression occurred in 1% to 3% of those receiving venlafaxine. In a pooled analysis of placebo-controlled trials of antidepressants (n = 4,500 pediatrics and 77,000 adults), there was an increased risk for suicidal thoughts and behaviors in patients 24 years of age and younger receiving an antidepressant versus placebo, with considerable variation in the risk of suicidality among drugs. The difference in the absolute risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors across different indications was highest in those with major depression. No suicides occurred in any of the pediatric trials. These studies did not show an increase in the risk of suicidal thoughts and behavior with antidepressant use in patients over 24 years of age; there was a reduction in risk with antidepressant use in patients aged 65 and older. Caregivers and/or patients should immediately notify the prescriber of changes in behavior or suicidal ideation during venlafaxine treatment.

    Headline
    cheilitis, colitis, constipation, diarrhea, dysgeusia, dyspepsia, dysphagia, eructation, esophagitis, flatulence, gastritis, GI obstruction, gingivitis, glossitis, hemorrhoids, hypersalivation, nausea, oral ulceration, pancreatitis, parotitis, peptic ulcer, proctitis, stomatitis, teeth grinding (bruxism), tongue discoloration, vomiting, xerostomia
    General Information
    Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse effects of venlafaxine are commonly reported and some appear to be dose-dependent; some GI side effects may improve with continued use. Nausea was reported in 37% (vs. 11% placebo) in clinical trials of IR venlafaxine and in 32.6% to 58% of patients in a fixed-dose study comparing 75, 225, and 375 mg/day with placebo. Xerostomia (dry mouth) was reported in 22% of patients receiving IR venlafaxine and 12% to 16% of patients during ER venlafaxine trials. Xerostomia sometimes resulted in discontinuation of therapy, and, over a period of 6 weeks, patients did not adapt to this side effect with continued use. Other GI adverse effects reported during clinical trials of IR venlafaxine at rates higher than with placebo included constipation (15%), diarrhea (8%), vomiting (6%), dyspepsia (5%), and flatulence (3%). During clinical trials of ER venlafaxine for various psychiatric indications, nausea (21% to 35%), constipation (8% to 10%), vomiting (3% to 5%) were reported. In studies of ER venlafaxine for social anxiety disorder, diarrhea (8%) and dyspepsia (7%) were reported more often than with placebo. Dysgeusia was reported in 2% of patients (IR venlafaxine) and 1% or more of patients (venlafaxine ER). Eructation and increased appetite were reported in 1% or more of patients. GI adverse effects occurring in 1% or less of patients during pre-marketing evaluations of venlafaxine include teeth grinding (bruxism), colitis, dysphagia, tongue edema, esophagitis, gastritis, gastroenteritis, peptic ulcer (reported as gastrointestinal ulcer), gingivitis, glossitis, hemorrhoids, oral moniliasis, stomatitis, oral ulceration. GI effects occurring in less than 0.1% of patients in pre-marketing evaluations of venlafaxine include cheilitis, duodenitis, esophageal spasm, ileitis, GI obstruction, parotitis, periodontitis, proctitis, hypersalivation, soft stools, and tongue discoloration. Postmarketing reports indicate that pancreatitis has occurred; the incidence is unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.

    Headline
    hypertension, hypotension, orthostatic hypotension, preeclampsia
    General Information
    Venlafaxine has been associated with dose-related hypertension. Cases of hypertension that required immediate treatment have been reported. Venlafaxine can induce sustained dose-related hypertension (typically 10 to 15 mmHg). During pre-marketing studies with venlafaxine ER, the percentages of patients with various psychiatric diagnoses having sustained hypertension, defined as supine diastolic blood pressure (SDBP) of at least 90 mmHg and at least 10 mmHg above baseline during consecutive visits, were as follows: major depressive disorder (3%), generalized anxiety disorder (0.5%), social anxiety disorder (0.6%), and panic disorder (0.9%). The percentage of patients with sustained elevations in SDBP receiving venlafaxine IR was 3% of those taking less than 100 mg/day, 5% in those taking 101 mg/day to 200 mg/day, 7% in those taking 201 mg/day to 300 mg/day, and 13% of those taking more than 300 mg/day (2% for placebo). In trials, hypertension was recorded in 2.2% of patients receiving venlafaxine IR doses of 225 mg (n = 89) and 4.5% receiving 375 mg (n = 88). Across all clinical trials with ER venlafaxine, 1.4% of patients experienced an increase in SDBP of at least 15 mmHg compared to 0.9% of those receiving placebo. In clinical trials of IR venlafaxine, vasodilation occurred in 4% of patients (vs. 3% placebo) and postural or orthostatic hypotension was reported in 1%. Hypotension was reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluations of IR and ER venlafaxine. Regular monitoring of blood pressure is recommended for all patients. Pre-existing high blood pressure should be controlled prior to beginning treatment. Blood pressure increases may necessitate a dose reduction or possible discontinuation of venlafaxine. Exposure to venlafaxine in mid to late pregnancy may increase the risk of preeclampsia. In retrospective cohort studies based on claims data, increased preeclampsia risk was observed at venlafaxine doses of 75 mg/day and above with a duration of more than 30 days. Another study that assessed venlafaxine exposure in gestational weeks 10 to 20 and the incidence of preeclampsia found an increased risk at doses of 150 mg/day and above.

    Headline
    angina, atrial fibrillation, AV block, bradycardia, bundle-branch block, cardiomyopathy, chest pain (unspecified), edema, elevated creatine kinase, heart failure, myocardial infarction, pallor, peripheral vasoconstriction, phlebitis, QT prolongation, sinus tachycardia, supraventricular tachycardia (SVT), syncope, torsade de pointes, ventricular fibrillation, ventricular tachycardia
    General Information
    In various venlafaxine trials comparing IR or ER formulations to placebo, analysis of ECG changes showed statistically significant and dose-related changes in heart rate (relative increase of 3 to 8.5 beats/min). Tachycardia was reported in 2% of patients receiving venlafaxine IR compared to no patients receiving placebo. In clinical trials for various indications,QT prolongation was documented with a mean increase in QTc of 1.5 to 4.7 msec for ER venlafaxine vs. a decrease of 0.7 to 1.9 msec for placebo. Chest pain (unspecified) and edema were reported in more than 1% of patients in premarketing trials of both venlafaxine formulations. Other cardiovascular effects reported in 0.1 to 1% of patients in clinical trials (IR and ER formulations) include angina pectoris, arrhythmia, extrasystoles, syncope, bradycardia (ER formulation), thrombo-phlebitis (IR product), and peripheral vascular disorder (mainly cold hands and feet, similar to Raynaud's phenomena with peripheral vasoconstriction). The following events were reported rarely (less than 0.1%) during pre-marketing evaluations: aortic aneurysm, arteritis, first degree AV block, bigeminy, bradycardia (IR formulation), bundle-branch block, capillary fragility, mitral valve and circulatory disturbance, cerebral ischemia, coronary artery disease, congestive heart failure, myocardial infarction, mucocutaneous hemorrhage, sinus arrhythmia (ER formulation), thrombo-phlebitis (ER formulation), and pallor. Postmarketing reports with venlafaxine include elevated creatine kinase, cardiac arrhythmias (e.g., atrial fibrillation ventricular, extrasystoles, ventricular fibrillation, ventricular tachycardia, supraventricular tachycardia (SVT)), QT prolongation and torsade de pointes. Takotsubo cardiomyopathy has also been reported during postmarketing experience with venlafaxine. Electrocardiogram (ECG) changes such as QT interval prolongation, bundle branch block, QRS prolongation, sinus tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, and sinus bradycardia have occurred in conjunction with overdose of venlafaxine in some cases.

    Headline
    anorexia, growth inhibition, weight loss
    General Information
    Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse effects of venlafaxine are very common. In clinical trials of IR venlafaxine, anorexia (11% IR, 8% to 17% ER) was frequently reported and resulted in venlafaxine discontinuation for some patients. Over several weeks, patients may adapt to some GI effects of venlafaxine. Venlafaxine also leads to a dose-dependent weight loss in some patients. Weight loss occurring with venlafaxine is not always associated with treatment-induced anorexia. In adult major depression disorder (MDD) clinical trials, about 5% of body weight was lost in 7% of patients receiving venlafaxine ER. The discontinuation rate due to weight loss in depression trials was low, roughly 0.1% of patients. Weight loss in adult general anxiety disorder (GAD) trials (lasting up to 6 months) was similar to the weight loss in depression trials. Weight loss in panic and social anxiety disorder trials was slightly less (incidence: 4%) than that seen in depression and GAD trials. Weight loss has also been noted with pediatric use of venlafaxine. In 8-week studies for MDD or GAD, venlafaxine-treated pediatric patients lost an average of 0.45 kg while placebo-treated patients gained an average of 0.77 kg. Eighteen percent of children in the venlafaxine group lost 3.5% or more of body weight compared to 3.6% of the placebo group, a significant difference. In social anxiety disorder (SAD) studies, pediatric patients lost an average 0.75 kg. Additionally, 47% of venlafaxine-treated children lost 3.5% or more of their body weight compared to 14% of placebo patients. Six-month studies in children with MDD have revealed that longer term weight gains are less than expected based on data from age- and sex-matched peers. The difference between observed weight gain and expected weight gain was larger for children than for adolescents. Changes in height (e.g., growth inhibition) have been noted in short and long-term pediatric studies. In an 8-week study, pediatric patients (ages 6 to 17 years) grew an average of 0.3 cm (n = 122), while placebo-treated patients grew an average of 1.0 cm (n = 132); the difference was significant. This difference in height was most notable in patients 12 years of age. During a 16-week, placebo-controlled SAD study, both the venlafaxine XR-treated (n = 109) and the placebo-treated (n = 112) patients each grew an average of 1 cm. A 6-month open-label study also noted height increases that were less than expected based on data from age- and sex-matched peers; however, the long-term consequences are not known. Clinicians should regularly monitor height and weight changes in pediatric patients receiving venlafaxine.

    Headline
    ejaculation dysfunction, impotence (erectile dysfunction), libido decrease, orgasm dysfunction
    General Information
    Venlafaxine therapy may cause sexual dysfunction and reported incidences are higher than with placebo. Libido decrease occurred in 2% of patients treated with IR venlafaxine and 3% to 8% of patients treated with ER venlafaxine for various psychiatric indications. Ejaculation dysfunction (12%, males), impotence (erectile dysfunction) (up to 6%, males), and orgasm dysfunction (2%, women) were reported during IR venlafaxine studies. Impotence (4% to 5%, males), abnormal ejaculation (8% to 19%, males), and orgasm dysfunction (anorgasmia or delayed orgasm; 2% to 5%, women) occurred during ER venlafaxine trials for various indications. Prescribers should discuss sexual function prior to initiating treatment with venlafaxine and throughout treatment and obtain a detailed history and timeline of any changes in sexual function to determine whether the changes are medication-related or may be attributed to the underlying psychiatric disorder. Clinicians should also discuss management strategies and treatment options with patients.

    Headline
    flushing, hyperhidrosis, night sweats
    General Information
    During controlled clinical trials of venlafaxine IR, an increase in sweating (hyperhidrosis) was reported in 12% of patients. The incidence appears to be dose-dependent and was reported in 6.7% of patients receiving 75 mg/day, 12.4% receiving 225 mg/day, and 19.3% of patients receiving 375 mg/day of venlafaxine IR. Among various psychiatric dosages of venlafaxine ER studied in clinical trials, sweating was reported in 10% to 14% of patients. Sweating was a common adverse event leading to discontinuation of venlafaxine in placebo-controlled trials. Flushing (hot flush) or vasodilatation has been reported in trials evaluating venlafaxine ER for various psychiatric indications (3% to 4%). In a separate trial evaluating social anxiety disorder, vasodilation (flushing) was found to be dose-related, occurring twice as often in patients receiving venlafaxine 150 to 225 mg/day compared to those receiving 75 mg/day. Postmarketing reports include night sweats.

    Headline
    blurred vision, cataracts, conjunctivitis, diplopia, mydriasis, ocular hypertension, photophobia, xerophthalmia
    General Information
    Blurred vision was reported in clinical trials of IR venlafaxine (6%) and venlafaxine ER (4% to 5%) for various psychiatric indications and at rates higher than with placebo. Abnormality of accomodation was reported in 1% or more of patients, mydriasis was reported in 2% (IR venlafaxine) and more than 1% (ER venlafaxine) during pre-marketing evaluations. Ophthalmic effects reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluations include cataracts, conjunctivitis, corneal lesion, diplopia, dry eyes (xerophthalmia), and photophobia. Angle closure glaucoma and ocular hypertension have been noted postmarketing.

    Headline
    seizures
    General Information
    In major depressive disorder trials with venlafaxine IR, seizures were reported at various doses in 0.26% (8 cases out of 3,082 patients). No seizures occurred during pre-marketing clinical trials with venlafaxine ER for depression, generalized anxiety disorder, or social anxiety disorder. The addition of venlafaxine to established clozapine therapy may result in seizure activity. Rapid withdrawal of venlafaxine monotherapy has also been associated with seizures. Venlafaxine should be discontinued in any patient who develops seizures.

    Headline
    serotonin syndrome
    General Information
    Postmarketing reports indicate that serotonin syndrome has occurred during administration of venlafaxine. Serotonin syndrome has been reported during use of SNRIs alone, during concurrent use of other medications known to increase serotonin levels, or during SNRI overdose. Symptoms may include nausea, vomiting, sedation, dizziness, diaphoresis, facial flush, mental status changes, myoclonia, restlessness, shivering, and elevated blood pressure. If serotonin syndrome becomes evident during treatment, the SNRI and any other serotonergic agents should be discontinued and appropriate medical treatment should be initiated.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      serotonin syndrome
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    neuroleptic malignant syndrome
    General Information
    Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) has been reported during postmarketing use of venlafaxine. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) is a potentially life-threatening complication that may include symptoms such as hyperthermia, muscle rigidity, autonomic instability, and mental status changes. Concurrent use of antipsychotics or other dopamine antagonists with venlafaxine may increase the potential for NMS to occur. In one case report, the initiation of venlafaxine in a patient taking medications known to cause NMS developed symptoms consistent with NMS or serotonin syndrome, which can rarely, in its most severe form, resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome. After discontinuation of all psychiatric medications and treatment for NMS, the patient recovered and was able to reinitiate neuroleptic treatment without further problems. Symptoms consistent with NMS also occurred in a pediatric patient who may have been receiving methylphenidate concurrently with venlafaxine. If NMS becomes evident during treatment, venlafaxine and any nonessential antidopaminergic agents should be discontinued and appropriate medical treatment should be initiated.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      neuroleptic malignant syndrome
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    hyponatremia, SIADH
    General Information
    Hyponatremia was reported rarely (less than 0.1%) during pre-marketing evaluation of venlafaxine. Serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), including venlafaxine, may cause hyponatremia, which is frequently the result of the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). In some cases, serum sodium levels less than 110 mmol/L have been reported; however, the adverse effect appeared reversible upon discontinuation of the causative SNRI. Elderly patients, those receiving diuretics or prone to becoming dehydrated, and those who are otherwise volume depleted (e.g., hypovolemia) appear to be at greatest risk. Hyponatremia may manifest as headache, difficulty concentrating, memory impairment, confusion, weakness, and unsteadiness which may result in falls. Severe manifestations include hallucinations, fainting, seizure, coma, respiratory arrest, and death. Symptomatic hyponatremia may require discontinuation of the SNRI, as well as implementation of the appropriate medical interventions. Post-marketing reports indicate that SIADH has occurred. The incidence is unknown and a causal relationship to the drug has not been established.

    Headline
    bleeding, ecchymosis, GI bleeding, hematemesis, melena, petechiae, platelet dysfunction, prolonged bleeding time
    General Information
    Platelet dysfunction (i.e., impaired platelet aggregation) may occur during treatment with serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) due to platelet serotonin depletion, possibly increasing the risk of a bleeding complication (e.g., GI bleeding, ecchymoses, epistaxis, hematomas, petechiae, hemorrhage). Rectal bleeding and melena were reported infrequently (0.1% to 1%). GI bleeding, hematemesis, prolonged bleeding time, petechiae (petechial rash), and bleeding gums were rarely (less than 0.1%) reported during pre-marketing evaluation of venlafaxine. Ecchymosis was reported in more than 1% of patients during clinical trials. Elevations in prothrombin time, activated partial thromboplastin and INR values have been reported postmarketing when venlafaxine was added to established warfarin therapy, although overt bleeding was not reported. An increased risk of bleeding complications is possible in patients receiving antiplatelet or anticoagulant medications concurrently with venlafaxine.

Myopathy, muscle cramps, muscle spasms, and tendon rupture were reported rarely (0.1%). Rhabdomyolysis has been documented in spontaneous postmarketing reports with venlafaxine use.

    Headline
    hypercholesterolemia, hyperlipidemia
    General Information
    Hypercholesterolemia was an infrequent adverse event (occurring in 1 out of 100 to 1,000 patients); hyperlipidemia was reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients during the pre-marketing evaluation of venlafaxine. Venlafaxine has been associated with clinically important elevations in serum cholesterol (serum cholesterol 50 mg/dL or more from baseline and to a value of 261 mg/dL or higher). During a 12-month study, the mean elevation in serum cholesterol was 9.1 mg/dL. Mean increases in serum cholesterol concentrations of 1 to 7.9 mg/dL and 2.3 to 5.6 mg/dL have occurred in clinical trials with various dosages of venlafaxine ER for different psychiatric indications. The clinician may wish to monitor serum cholesterol levels at baseline and periodically during venlafaxine treatment.

    Headline
    cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatitis, jaundice, steatosis
    General Information
    Elevated hepatic enzymes (ALT, AST, GGT, and alkaline phosphatase) and probable drug-induced hepatitis were described in a case report of a woman aged 60 years who received venlafaxine 75 mg PO daily for vasomotor symptoms. After roughly one month of therapy, the patient presented with abdominal pain and an enlarged liver. Venlafaxine was discontinued, and after 1 week the liver function tests improved. After 4 weeks, rechallenge with 37.5 mg/day venlafaxine PO daily was attempted; however, liver enzymes also increased with the lower dose. Rare (less than 0.1%) reports of jaundice, hepatitis, cholecystitis, and cholelithiasis occurred during pre-marketing trials. Postmarketing reports of GGT elevation, LDH elevation, abnormal liver function tests, hepatic necrosis, hepatic failure, and fatty liver (steatosis) have occurred. Health care providers should be vigilant for the possibility of drug-induced hepatic events in patients receiving venlafaxine.

    Headline
    anosmia, apnea, cough, dysphonia, dyspnea, eosinophilic pneumonia, epistaxis, hemoptysis, hyperventilation, hypoventilation, hypoxia, laryngeal edema, laryngitis, laryngospasm, pulmonary embolism
    General Information
    Interstitial lung disease and eosinophilic pneumonia have been reported with venlafaxine during postmarketing use. Symptoms including cough, dyspnea, or chest discomfort should be promptly evaluated. Discontinuation of venlafaxine may become necessary. Increased cough (1% or more, venlafaxine ER), bronchitis (1% or more, venlafaxine IR) and dyspnea (0.1% to 1% or more, venlafaxine IR or ER) during venlafaxine clinical trials. Infrequent (0.1% to 1%) events included asthma, chest congestion, epistaxis, hyperventilation, laryngospasm, laryngitis, pneumonia and voice alteration (dysphonia). Atelectasis, hemoptysis, hypoventilation, hypoxia, laryngeal edema, pulmonary embolism, and sleep apnea have rarely been reported (less than 0.1%). Other postmarketing adverse reactions that have been reported include anosmia and hyposmia.

    Headline
    acne vulgaris, alopecia, anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema, contact dermatitis, Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), erythema multiforme, exfoliative dermatitis, hirsutism, maculopapular rash, photosensitivity, pruritus, psoriasis, rash, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis, urticaria, xerosis
    General Information
    Dermatologic and allergic reactions to venlafaxine have been reported. Rash (3%) and pruritus (1%) were reported during trials of venlafaxine IR and/or ER products. Infrequent (0.1% to 1%) events have included: acne vulgaris, alopecia, brittle nails, contact dermatitis, dry skin (xerosis), eczema, skin hypertrophy, maculopapular rash, photosensitivity, psoriasis, and urticaria. Rare (less than 0.1%) events included erythema nodosum, exfoliative dermatitis, lichenoid dermatitis, hair discoloration, skin discoloration, furunculosis, hirsutism, pustular rash, vesiculobullous rash (vesicular rash), seborrhea, skin atrophy or striae. Postmarketing reports indicate that angioedema, anaphylactoid reactions, toxic epidermal necrolysis (TEN), Stevens-Johnson Syndrome (SJS), and erythema multiforme have occurred. Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS) has also been reported during postmarketing use of venlafaxine according to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) Adverse Event Reporting System (FAERS). Manifestations of DRESS typically include pyrexia, rash, facial swelling, and/or lymph node involvement in conjunction with other organ system abnormalities including hepatitis, nephritis, hematologic abnormalities, myocarditis, or myositis. Eosinophilia is often present. Early manifestations of DRESS such as pyrexia and lymph node involvement may be present without evidence of a rash. Venlafaxine should be promptly discontinued and appropriate medical treatment should be initiated in patients presenting with hypersensitivity reactions, serious dermatologic events, or symptoms indicative of DRESS in whom an unrelated etiology cannot be identified.

      GSTerm Name
      Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS)
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    amenorrhea, anuria, breast discharge, breast enlargement, flank pain, glycosuria, gynecomastia, hypercalciuria, increased urinary frequency, mastalgia, menorrhagia, menstrual irregularity, nephrolithiasis, nocturia, oliguria, ovarian cyst, polyuria, prostatic hypertrophy, prostatitis, proteinuria, renal failure (unspecified), spontaneous fetal abortion, urinary incontinence, urinary retention, urinary urgency, vaginal bleeding, vaginitis
    General Information
    Genitourinary and reproductive effects reported during clinical trials of venlafaxine IR included increased urinary frequency (3%), impaired urination (2%), and urinary retention (1%). Menstrual irregularity (including metrorrhagia), vaginitis, prostatitis, and prostatic hypertrophy were reported in 1% or more of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of venlafaxine IR. Amenorrhea, menorrhagia, nocturia, breast pain (mastalgia), polyuria, urinary urgency, urinary retention, urinary incontinence, and vaginal bleeding were reported infrequently (0.1 to 1%). Rare reports (less than 0.1%) included anuria, spontaneous fetal abortion, breast discharge, breast enlargement or engorgement, endometriosis, fibrocystic breast, cervicitis, ovarian cyst, gynecomastia (male), kidney calculus or nephrolithiasis, kidney flank pain, female lactation, mastitis, menopause, oliguria, pyelonephritis, salpingitis, uterine bleeding, urolithiasis, uterine spasm and vaginal dryness. Albuminuria (proteinuria) and impaired micturition were reported in more than 1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of venlafaxine ER. Glycosuria and hypercalciuria were rarely reported (less than 0.1%). There have been postmarketing reports of renal failure (unspecified) with venlafaxine use.
